How socialisation, skill and standing out can contribute to grad success
2026-09-14

How socialisation, skill and standing out can contribute to grad success

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Accenture’s Barry O’Keeffe, Laoise Macken and Stephen Crothers discuss what graduates can expect from a career that starts with a development programme. Graduate programmes are an ideal way for students and recent graduates to develop their skills, a network and also make plans for their futures. Taking the chance to come into a company at an early stage of one’s career and experience a range of departments, roles and responsibilities can expose new professionals to career routes they perhaps hadn’t considered. It can also cement in their minds the areas that they want to give particular focus to. Moreover, it can create a welcoming environment that prioritises easing new employees in, so that the experience, while focused on learning, upskilling and career growth, is not overwhelming. Speaking to SiliconRepublic.com, Barry O’Keeffe, a business and technology integration analyst at Accenture, explained that part of his role at the organisation involves working as an AI engineer for a large client. He said, “We’re building out a data warehouse to monitor operational metrics for trust and safety data – so content moderation. My priority at the moment is developing an agentic system, which automatically handles incoming bugs raised within the warehouse.” The socialisation and camaraderie that can grow within the Accenture graduate programme is, for O’Keeffe, among its greatest characteristics. “One of my favourite parts of Accenture is that when you join in your first week, you’re assigned a buddy, who’s a current graduate, and a people lead, who’s at manager level,” he said. “The buddy you can go to during your first few weeks in your first year with all of the questions that you will have. The people lead is there to help with your career progression. So, any guidance that you need throughout your time at Accenture.” The same experience is true for management consulting analyst Laoise Macken, who said, “I now live with two people from my start group back in September and that’s not an uncommon thing that you hear from the analysts in Accenture. People get really close really quick.” According to senior manager and delivery lead Stephen Crothers, this level of guidance is key to building up a graduate so that they have the skills needed for a long and successful career. “I think guidance and mentorship is huge in our graduate programme, but I equally think that’s really important throughout any kind of career,” he said. “I’ve been in Accenture for nearly nine years now and I would say that every different point in my career, whether it be a big milestone or a promotion, can always be attributed down to one of the many mentors that I’ve had throughout that stage of my career.” Of the qualities he would expect in a professional thinking of joining the graduate programme, he said: “The things that we look for are both on the skills and the behaviour side. However, skills can be taught – behaviours can’t. “Some of the key pieces that I look for whenever I’m doing my interviews are that curiosity. Another thing is around proactivity. We want someone that’s proactive, that doesn’t need to be given a kind of a defined roadmap of tasks to do, but to have that self-starter mentality.” Macken added, “A skill that I’ve found useful in my role here at Accenture is being adaptable. Things move really quickly here, and our clients are at the forefront of AI and leaders in their industry, which means that priorities can change quickly, and you need to be comfortable diving headfirst into the work around you. “So I think that people that are open-minded, hardworking and adaptable really thrive here. What surprised me most about the graduate programme is how strong the analyst community is.” Macken added: “Another skill that I’ve found really useful in my experience at Accenture has been communication. The teams that we work on are very cross-functional and range across a very wide level of seniority.” As a result, she finds that she is able to communicate confidently while also maintaining who she is as an individual and developing genuine relationships. She advised anyone considering a role as a graduate to have a ‘sponge-like’ mentality to soak up new skills and advice. “The people around you are absolute experts in their field and have such depth of industry knowledge that there’s not a single person that you’ll meet here that won’t be able to teach you something at all levels – analyst or MD. So yeah, forging genuine connections with people and asking questions and really trying to absorb as much as you can.” Be prepared for any interview, said Crothers, “then do more preparation” to really think about the questions you will be asked, the kind of answers you will give and how you express yourself. “That’s what helps you build out, I suppose, that skillset to take you to a place where, again, it would be unrealistic to not succeed.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. For reasons unknown to the public, the founder and CEO of Automattic Matt Mullenweg was briefly ousted from his position last week. He was reinstated a few days later. Last Wednesday (9 September), Mullenweg posted to a company-wide Slack channel that Automattic’s chief financial officer Mark Davies had “conspired” with other board members, including founding CEO Toni Schneider (who is the current CEO of Bluesky), to put him on a paid leave of absence. The board voted Davies as interim CEO and told publications that it had full confidence that he would be able to “execute against the company’s priorities”. However, by Friday (11 September), Mullenweg seemed to be back in his position, according to newer Slack messages verified by company employees. “A lot happened in the past 48 hours that we need to sort out, and I hope much of it was a misunderstanding, because I have huge respect and regard for those involved,” he wrote on Slack. And in a statement, Automattic confirmed with the media that Mullenweg is back as the chairperson and CEO of his company “with full support of the board”. “If you search online you can see many top executives and Automatticians supporting him as well”, the spokesperson told TechCrunch. Mullenweg, according to his posts on X, seems to be taking his brief ousting in stride. Last Friday, in a series of posts, he seemingly joked that this was the fifth attempt at a coup in his company. “Founder advice: If you don’t have a coup attempt every few years, you’re not hiring strong enough leaders,” he said. In a separate post, he wrote, “Boards are never boring,” with a ‘laughing-crying’ emoji accompanying the statement. Automattic is best known as the creator behind WordPress.com. It also owns the microblogging platform Tumblr, WooCommerce – a plugin for WordPress – and the online survey and quiz conductor CrowdSignal, formerly known as PollDaddy. Recovery readiness a missing link in cyber resilience, finds report
2026-09-14

Recovery readiness a missing link in cyber resilience, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Michelle Harris. Image: Joe Conroy Photography Triangle’s independent Cyber Recovery Readiness survey found that prolonged business outages are a major risk for organisations. According to new research based on Triangle Technology Services’ independent Cyber Recovery Readiness survey, organisations are facing significant challenges in preparing for and recovering from cyberattacks. The company surveyed 130 IT and security decision-makers to explore how ready companies are for a cyber threat, but also how they might navigate the aftermath. What was found is that prolonged business outages are a major risk factor for organisations, particularly as frontier AI accelerates cyber threats. Of those who participated, 64pc of companies were found to be missing a key risk remediation, 62pc confirmed that their organisation has isolated backups, but only 36pc could validate the integrity of that data when needed. Commenting on the results of the report, Michelle Harris, the director of cyber recovery at Triangle, said, “Many organisations are investing heavily in AI for prevention of cyber attacks, but far fewer regularly test their ability to recover from a breach. “Recovery readiness is now a critical business issue, and this survey provides valuable insight into how prepared organisations really are when disruption occurs.” Additional findings indicated that, of the participating organisations, 51pc have a clean recovery environment and 62pc have a dedicated, coordinated recovery team. Almost half of companies have tested or rehearsed their recovery in the last year. “While threat awareness is real and growing, it has not yet translated into the right kind of action,” said Harris. “The National Cyber Security Centre in Britain changed their focus from prevention to recovery.  They’re saying organisations should assume there will be an event.  That hasn’t filtered through to a lot of organisations, where they are still focusing on prevention, which they do still have to do, of course.” She added, “As cyberattacks become more sophisticated, businesses need to assume breaches will occur and ensure, through regular testing, they can recover operations quickly and securely. “Organisations that have implemented mature cyber recovery capabilities know in advance of a breach what data they will recover, where they will be recovering to and how long it will take, so there is no guesswork involved when everyone is under pressure.” In June, the Eir Business Hidden Cost of Cyber Risk report found that on average, cyberattacks are costing Irish small and medium-sized enterprises up to €3.4bn annually. It was discovered that the greatest impact is not from large-scale, one-off breaches, but frequent day-to-day cybersecurity-related disruptions that are driving losses for many Irish companies. IVCA raises alarm over foreign VC dominating Irish SME rounds

IVCA raises alarm over foreign VC dominating Irish SME rounds

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
‘These levels are flashing red warning signals’, says IVCA chairperson Richard Watson. The Irish Venture Capital Association (IVCA) has raised concerns over the influence of international venture capital funding in Irish domestic companies, as foreign contributions represented 82pc of the total investment in Irish SMEs in the first half of 2026. “These levels are flashing red warning signals as they represent some of the highest on record and emphasise our over-exposure to overseas investors,” said IVCA chairperson Richard Watson of the VenturePulse survey data published today (13 September). Global geopolitical instability, including the war in Iran, as well as the significant concentration of US capital in blockbuster AI deals could well impact future international VC investment into Ireland, he said. Turbulent geopolitical conditions caused a 71pc fall-off in international investment in Q4 last year, leading to a 46pc decline in total funding for the quarter, while contributing to a 23pc drop-off in funding for the year. The “rollercoaster” year for funding was attributed to an “undoubted Trump effect”, after uncertainty caused by tariffs led to the worst second quarter for 10 years. Meanwhile, VC funding of AI giants has dominated the global charts in recent years, led by Anthropic and OpenAI – two of the biggest private companies in the world that between them took 43pc of global start-up funding in the first half of 2026. Total venture capital funding into Irish SMEs fell 10pc to €578.4m in the first half of 2026, while the second quarter marked year-on-year growth of more than 200pc to €356.7m. That significant jump, however, is in comparison to the same period last year – which was the worst in 10 years. Of the more than half-a-billion euros in funding raised by Irish SMEs in the first half of this year, deals of more than €30m grew by 3pc, while all funding brackets under €5m dropped. Comparatively, in the second quarter, deals crossing €30m grew by a considerable 570pc, while there were no deals in the €10m to €30m range. Two companies – Dublin fintech provider Fonoa and Belfast cybersecurity firm Cloudsmith – accounted for nearly 44pc of the total raised in the second quarter, led majorly by American venture firms. Other major rounds in Q2 included drone delivery company Manna, which raised €43m; fleet safety tech provider CameraMatics, which raised €49m; and AI space-tech Ubotica. Smaller deals included women’s health-tech Peri’s €100,000 round and AI accessibility start-up Nexus Inclusion’s €1.5m raise. The data highlights the “extent to which the dearth of domestic sources of funding is limiting capital formation”, said IVCA director-general Sarah-Jane Larkin. Life sciences and fintech companies led the way in the first half of the year, both accounting for 26pc of total funding, followed by cybersecurity and software at 14pc each. Meanwhile, AI and machine learning represented 7pc. “This does not mean we are being left behind in AI,” commented Larkin. “It is unrealistic to expect a small nation like Ireland to produce the giant platform and frontier AI leaders of the US. But AI is now embedded in the products and services of the vast majority of local VC-backed SMEs.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Chinese Government Dismisses Tech Billionaire Calls for an AI Slowdown as “Fear-Mongering”

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
In a whiplash-inducing turn away from the mythical quest to build human-level artificial intelligence, Western tech billionaires like Dario Amodei, Sam Altman, and Elon Musk are now joining hands to call for a massive slowdown of the tech’s development. The commentariat is divided on what exactly this means — for some, it’s proof-positive of a genuine threat that AI could spin out of control and exterminate everybody. To others it’s a smokescreen, a thin cover provided by AI CEOs who are desperate for a government bailout to avoid popping the AI investment bubble. If you ask lawmakers in Beijing, the whole thing is a bunch of nonsense. In a routine press scrum hosted by China’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs, government spokesperson Guo Jiakun addressed a laundry list of topics, from President Xi Jinping’s recent trip to New Delhi to recovery efforts around the devastating mudslides in Nepal. One of the questions, posed by a Reuters journalist, concerned whether the ministry had any comment on calls from American tech billionaires to slow down AI development. “AI is a consequential technology for the wellbeing of all humanity,” Guo replied. “All parties should jointly promote the open and inclusive development of AI for good and for all. Fear-mongering, confrontation and vicious competition will only hamper efforts toward sound global AI governance, which serves no one’s interest.” Compared to the weight given to other topics, the ministry’s statement on the Western AI debacle was decidedly terse. While talk of an AI slowdown is roiling US markets and trending on social media, it seems like it’s barely a blip on Beijing’s radar. The virtual hand-waving follows the decidedly disinterested stance Chinese policymakers have taken toward the US approach to AI development, which for years has hinged on building toward human-level artificial general intelligence. As research fellow at the partially US-funded Brookings Institute Kyle Chan wrote in February, Beijing’s concept of “AGI” is wildly different from the one used across the pond. While US policymakers and industry leaders treat the tech as a race to AGI where one winner takes all, their Chinese counterparts aren’t in any rush. The US might get there first, the idea goes, but like electric vehicles, cancer treatments, and smartphones, Chinese industry can quickly make up the difference. “Let’s look at the history of the internet. It started in the US, but China quickly caught up and led in consumer applications — WeChat is a world-class example,” professor emeritus at the Hong Kong University of Science and Technology Yang Qiang said during a panel at the AGI Next Summit in January. “AI is similar — it’s a tool, and Chinese ingenuity will push it to the limit.” More on China and AI: Top Chinese Court Issues Sweeping Legal Crackdown on AI Deepfakes

Amazon Has Become Pathetically Reliant on Food Stamps to Power Its Empire

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
After over two decades of operation, Amazon Prime has become the backbone of American commerce, more akin to a public utility than a private company. Its logistics network has blown past the US Postal Service in parcel volume, delivering crucial goods to small business owners, trade workers, and teachers at record speed. And like any public utility, Amazon Prime comes with a public price tag — a massive state subsidy paid by taxpayers around the country. Blistering new reporting by Fortune revealed that the number of Amazon workers receiving government assistance via Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) and Medicaid has nearly tripled between 2020 and 2025, a stunning rise for any group, let alone workers employed at one of the wealthiest companies in the world. It comes as the megacorporation posted a massive rise in annual profit over the same period, from $11.6 billion in 2020 to $77.7 billion in 2025. Those federal assistance numbers, obtained by the independent US Government Accountability Office, found that 12,346 Amazon workers across 11 states were getting help affording basic groceries through SNAP. A further 11,338 in those states rely on Medicaid. The federal assistant amounts to a de facto subsidy of Amazon’s labor force, allowing the company executives to rake in record profits while paying its workers starvation wages. If it weren’t for these government programs, in other words, thousands of Amazon’s lowest-paid workers wouldn’t be able to afford to make ends meet. Though Amazon was far from the only company whose full time employees are struggling, the GAO survey found that Amazon was consistently among the very top employers of adult SNAP and Medicaid enrollees. Other top offenders include Walmart, ride-share platform companies like Uber, and chain stores like McDonald’s and Dollar General. Beyond Amazon, the survey observes the grim reality of working class life in the wealthiest nation on Earth. Overall, the report points to the fact that nearly 14 million working adults were enrolled in Medicaid in 2024, while 10.6 million received SNAP benefits. Of those receiving government aid for groceries, some 71.3 percent — about 7.5 million people — worked 35 hours or more per week. “What this analysis really points to is the large number of people who have very low incomes and continue to have very low income,” GAO director for education, workforce, and income security issues Kathryn Larin told Fortune. “I mean, these are families that are really barely able to make ends meet, and yet they are working, and they are working a lot.” More on Amazon: Amazon Investigating Its Own Employees for Daring to Question AI Data Centers

The Winner of Elon Musk’s AI “Odyssey” Contest Will Make You Laugh Out Loud

The Cyclops Polyphemus is depicted with three eyes, which feels disqualifying. And just a few cuts later, the famously monocular giant is at least four times as large as he was before. The other creatures and gods look like something out of a mobile game; Poseidon emerges from the sea appearing conspicuously cartoonish and impossibly muscled, before smashing Odysseus’s ship and his crew of photorealistic, ripped men. Half the dialog is delivered in dramatic whispers with the same cadence. The fight scenes are cut to disguise the actual moments of impact, in the same way real B-movies do with aging action stars. There are some shots here and there that look convincing enough, if you can overlook their bland aesthetic. But this AI “film” suffers the same tragic flaw that all AI films do: their component clips fail to coalesce into a coherent whole. It doesn’t do much on the “historically accurate” front, either; some of the Greek soldiers are clearly wearing the armor donned by Roman legionnaires. Nonetheless, its creator Nem Perez declared that “this is ‘The Odyssey’ as Homer intended,” before calling it his “most intricate work to date.” He also labeled the video, in oxymoronic fashion, a “5-minute epic.” This is "The Odyssey" as Homer intended. Nolan, Musk accused, “desecrated Homer and groveled on his knees just to meet the woke rules required to win an Oscar.” As such, he vowed that his AI “Grok Imagine will make a full-length movie of The Odyssey that is historically accurate and true to the art of Homer.” We’re still waiting for that full-length adaptation. But the short films that the contest has produced already make it painfully clear that AI video generators, and the “filmmakers” that embrace them, simply aren’t up to snuff. In all of the top three entries, Odysseus looks more or less like the same guy — some weird, looksmaxxer’s fantasy of a buff Antonio Banderas. Is this a fault of Grok or Grok users? Anthropic Boasts It Would Be Profitable if You Ignore How Much It Costs to Develop AI
2026-09-14

Anthropic Boasts It Would Be Profitable if You Ignore How Much It Costs to Develop AI

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Despite being one of the world leaders in AI development, the private firm Anthropic has yet to post a quarterly profit. That operating deficit isn’t for a lack of trying — and, as the company tells it, could be reversing very soon, at least if you’re willing to swallow one massive caveat. According to new reporting by the Financial Times, the company behind the Claude told a select group of trusted investors that its adjusted operating income (AOI) is set to be positive for the second consecutive quarter. AOI is an accounting technique that strips out non-recurring costs, giving backers a very specific look under a company’s hood. Posting a profit on AOI is very different from posting a profit on net income, otherwise known as the “bottom line,” under which Anthropic remains billions of dollars in the red. Aside from the fact that Anthropic is not about to turn a real profit anytime soon, it may still be a noteworthy signal. The AI world is currently awash in fear-mongering over AI safety, a phenomena some analysts say could signal a turn away from the race-to-the-bottom to build artificial general intelligence toward a more reasonable — if less ambitious — embrace of AI as it currently is. While it remains to be seen how these narratives and business realities play out in practice, if the top American AI companies choose to stop and let the dust settle, Anthropic may very well find itself ahead of its competition. Though Anthropic is still far underwater, companies like ChatGPT developer OpenAI are somehow even deeper. As lead AI analyst at the firm SemiAnalysis Joey Brookhart told the FT, “if you continue to operate at these margins and growth rates, it will be so hard to compete [with Anthropic] because they have so much [in the way of computing resources].” Still, there are some questions in the AI lab’s story that have yet to be answered. For one thing, this profitability forecast — a flattering projection conveniently leaked to the financial press at a pivotal moment — comes in place of a highly anticipated public prospectus which the company was expected to make public sometime last week. Anthropic may still be planning to release the prospectus soon, but it’s worth noting the company has chosen to lead with spectacle and innuendo, as opposed to the formal legal document. For months, Anthropic has been teasing a public stock offering that hasn’t yet materialized, and it remains to be seen whether the latest slowdown talk will impact that timeline. In fact, there’s reason to believe this song and dance is all part of the lead-up to the AI lab’s IPO, as investors whip themselves into a frenzy about AI safety. As Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner said in a post on X-formerly-Twitter over the weekend, “Anthropic will IPO” anyway, because there is “huge appetite to invest in the AI leaders,” especially those which are seen as bringing “even more transparency, scrutiny, accountability” into the AI space (at least as far as Gerstner’s investments are concerned.) More on Anthropic: New Anthropic Ad Implies AI Could Kill Us All

Bernie Sanders Proposes Banning Superintelligent AI and Imprisoning Developers for 20 Years
2026-09-14

Bernie Sanders Proposes Banning Superintelligent AI and Imprisoning Developers for 20 Years

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
While the US appears to be further away from meaningful AI regulations than ever before, lawmakers certainly have plenty of ideas on how the country could go about setting meaningful rules. It’s a particularly precarious moment as fears over rogue AI models wreaking havoc and endangering humanity continue to mount. Even tech leaders from the majority of frontier AI labs have started to coalesce around the idea that the industry has to slow down the development of the tech to get ahead of a possible catastrophe. Vermont senator Bernie Sanders (VT-D) is intensifying his calls for a ban on the development of so-called superintelligent AIs — and he’s willing to go a lot further than some of his like-minded peers. “Dario Amodei, Elon Musk and Sam Altman now agree that we must slow down the development of AI and ‘pace the frontier,'” he tweeted last week. “That’s a start, but it’s not enough.” Sanders was referring to a lengthy essay titled “We Must Pace the Frontier,” in which Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei argued that the industry should open its doors to third-party evaluators, establish common safety standards, and coordinate with other democracies around the world to enforce these standards. “When you are racing towards a cliff, you don’t just ease up on the gas pedal,” Sanders added. “You hit the brakes.” This month, the lawmaker announced the “Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act,” which is legislation designed to “stop AI oligarchs from building machines humans cannot control.” The bill, if enacted, would stop AI development “until a federal regulator has established safety rules.” It would also set new penalties for anybody attempting to circumvent these rules. AI developers who try to build superintelligent AI anyway could be “subject to not more than 20 years in prison, which is similar to existing penalties related to unlawfully developing nuclear weapons.” Sanders has come up with plenty of other radical ideas to stop the AI industry from concentrating immense amounts of wealth. In June, he proposed creating a “one-time 50 percent tax” on the stock of AI companies, and transferring the money into a public fund to be managed by the government. Lawmakers became particularly concerned after a group of OpenAI AI models hacked the systems of Hugging Face, a pivotal cybersecurity failing that heightened calls to slow the development of AI. A Senate subcommittee has since announced that it’s looking into OpenAI and its response to the incident, as Axios reported last week. It’s a strange moment in time. While Sanders continues to threaten AI CEOs with regulatory action, those same executives appear open to having the government set the rules. But whether lawmakers will ever be able to find consensus remains unclear. The executive branch, for one, has demonstrated little interest in an AI slowdown. Earlier today, president Donald Trump mocked the very concept in a Truth Social post on Monday, arguing that there was a “SICK conspiracy going on against AI and Data Centers” without offering any evidence. The Planet Mercury Appears to Have Shrunken Drastically, “Like a Balloon Left Out in the Cold”
2026-09-14

The Planet Mercury Appears to Have Shrunken Drastically, “Like a Balloon Left Out in the Cold”

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Scientists say that the planet Mercury has been actively shrinking over the billions of years that it’s orbited the Sun, a process that’s leaving cracked-up wrinkles behind like an old apple. At just one-third the size of Earth, it’s already the smallest planet in our solar system — it’s larger than Pluto, but Pluto is no longer classified as a planet — in a title it’s bound to hold on to as its continues to diminish in physical size. Now, as detailed in a new paper by a team of scientists from the German Aerospace Center’s (DLR) Institute of Space Research, the oddball planet’s shrivelling could be occurring at an even faster-than-expected rate, losing up to 14.5 miles of total diameter since it formed billions of years ago. That’s anywhere from ten to 30 percent more than previously thought. The scientists suggest that debris from impact craters may have hidden this accelerated shrinking, a process largely thought to be the result of Mercury running out of heat — “like a balloon left out in the cold,” per an accompanying press release. The findings could help scientists get a better sense of what’s going on inside the planet and how it evolved over time. “More shrinking means Mercury could have a larger metal core, less light elements like silicon mixed into the metal core, or a higher starting temperature,” DLR planetary scientist Gaku Nishiyama explained in a statement. “Every planet is an odd little duck that has a story to tell,” Brown University planetary geoscientist Sam Birch, who was not involved in the study, told the New York Times. “If you can understand how fast Mercury is shrinking, you can understand how fast it’s cooling, what size it started at and what it’s made out of.” Mercury’s outermost layer of hard rock has been resisting — or at least trying to resist — this deflation, causing its surface to crack and “wrinkle.” It’s a rough surface lined with steep cliffs known as scarps, as well as ridges that make getting a broader sense of how much it’s shrinking overall difficult. By aggregating previous maps of the planet’s surface, the team found evidence that the roughest areas have fewer wrinkles, suggesting that impact debris is “obscuring shortening structures,” according to Nishiyama. Their newly revised rates of shrinking also happen to better reflect previous physics-based predictions. Fortunately, we’re about to have a close look at the deflating planet. The European Space Agency and Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency’s BepiColombo mission, which launched in 2018, will enter Mercury’s orbit in November, if all goes according to plan — becoming only the second spacecraft to do so in human history. More on Mercury: Mercury Has an Eleven-Mile-Thick Layer of Diamonds, Scientists Find

Cornelis is part of a new wave of AI infrastructure companies emerging to break apart Nvidia’s market dominance, piece by piece (or chip by chip, one could say). Amazon Prime Video takes on TikTok with short-form news clips

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Amazon Prime Video is coming after TikTok with the addition of short-form news clips. The company said on Monday that it’s expanding its news coverage to include on-demand news clips offering both local and national stories. These will be available on TVs and other living room devices, but will soon roll out to the web and Prime Video’s mobile apps, the company said. The clips will be available in the News destination within the Prime Video app in its Trending Topics and the Local News carousels. They join Prime Video’s existing local, national, and world news coverage from a number of major providers, which are available to everyone in the U.S. regardless of whether they have a Prime subscription. Amazon could not yet confirm whether the clips on mobile would be oriented for vertical video or not, only that they would be considered short-form content on iOS and Android. While TikTok is better known as an entertainment destination, it’s also, along with Instagram, increasingly a place where younger people get their news. Gen Z in particular often watches news creators and influencers on short-form apps, including YouTube Shorts. Amazon, which doesn’t have a true social destination of its own, is instead adding short-form video to Prime Video, in hopes of capturing Gen Z’s attention. But Gen Z viewers are drawn to these social apps not necessarily because of the length of the clips, but rather because of how the information is presented. Based on findings from Pew Research, these younger news consumers want short-form news content that “feels organic, not over-produced.” Prime Video is not the first streamer to add short-form clips to its content lineup. Peacock added support for short-form clips in March, followed by HBO Max and Netflix in July, then Disney+ in August. Combined, the updates represent an industrywide effort to meet viewers where they are — which is now often looking for entertainment in shorter bursts and consumable in moments or minutes instead of portions of hours or longer. TechCrunch
ClickFix attacks are tricking Mac and Windows users into hacking themselves
2026-09-14

ClickFix attacks are tricking Mac and Windows users into hacking themselves

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt If you clicked on an HBO Max ad on Reddit over the past week, you might want to check your computer for malware. These so-called “ClickFix” attacks have quickly become one of the rising cybersecurity threats of 2026, and they’re getting both sneakier and compromising people’s devices with greater frequency. Until recently, ClickFix attacks were a rarity, capitalizing on people searching the web for quick tech fixes. They have since evolved into a massive international effort to hack into people’s computers. The attacks involve fake websites, or legitimate websites that have been hacked, which display a message that appears to look like a CAPTCHA or an anti-bot checkbox. Once clicked, a prompt appears asking the user to perform a “check” to proceed, which gives instructions to copy and paste a string of text into the user’s Windows command prompt or Mac Terminal app. As soon as the user hits return, they unwittingly and instantly install info-stealing malware on their computer, capable of immediately stealing their passwords, access to their logged-in accounts, and crypto wallets. Since the user is working in the computer’s terminal, which lets them interact directly with the operating system using text-based commands, many of these attacks evade antivirus and security defense tools. Security researchers now say that the latest ClickFix campaign they’ve seen involved hackers posting fake ads on Reddit, linking to a page that looks like HBO Max but contains a ClickFix lure that tricks people into hacking themselves. The hackers compromised the official HBO Max’s account on Reddit that was then used to post hundreds of fake but real-looking adverts to the news-sharing site, according to security researchers at Hudson Rock and a thread on Reddit’s cybersecurity subreddit. It’s unclear how many people clicked on these fake ads or how many were ultimately compromised as a result. Warner Brothers Discovery, which owns HBO, did not respond to a request for comment. Reddit told TechCrunch it “recently learned that an HBO Max account authorized to run advertisements on Reddit was compromised and used to run ads containing malicious links,” and that the company locked the account and removed the ads. When asked, Reddit did not say how many users were targeted or clicked the malicious ads. While it’s typical for developers to run one-line snippets of code in their computer’s terminal, it’s less common for regular users to use the Command Prompt or PowerShell in Windows, or the Terminal in macOS. Companies that run fleets of Windows computers can block access to these features across the entire domain to prevent them from being exploited, per security researcher Kevin Beaumont. As noted by Ars Technica, a tool for Mac users called BlockBlock can also defend against attacks that try to trick Apple users into hacking themselves. TIME
The Emmys Squandered Beloved First-Time Host Mariska Hargitay
2026-09-15

The Emmys Squandered Beloved First-Time Host Mariska Hargitay

TIME | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Host Mariska Hargitay performs during the 78th Primetime Emmy Awards at Peacock Theater on September 14, 2026 in Los Angeles, California. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A day before Monday’s 78th Primetime Emmys, giddy headlines proclaimed that reigning king of basketball Jalen Brunson would be joining host Mariska Hargitay for a pre-taped intro bit. Well, it happened. We got a few seconds of the real-life pals steaming work outfits together, of all things. Then, suddenly, Hargitay was leading an “I Love Rock ‘n’ Roll” production number that had her singing about Guy Fieri on an airplane and cuddling up with Jean Smart in a hotel bed, in what felt uncomfortably like an ad for Marriott and United Airlines, whose logos were prominently placed. And then she was onstage at LA’s Peacock Theater, delivering a notably brief monologue. Spanning just eight minutes, the opening segment proved to be an accurate preview of a mediocre NBC and Peacock telecast that was by turns hasty, random, heavily reliant on big-name cameos, and not much of a showcase for Hargitay at all. It was a shame, not least because Hargitay was touted as the Emmys’ first female host since Jane Lynch in 2011. For many years after the Glee standout’s stint, the awards relied on a rotating cast of SNL and talk-show guys. Recently, however, with late night in decline, they’ve cast a wider net, and results have been all over the map. Following Anthony Anderson’s forgettable performance, we got an effortlessly charming turn from Eugene and Dan Levy—and then last year’s tedious, gimmick-freighted telecast, led by standup superstar Nate Bargatze. It wasn’t just gender that set Hargitay apart from those predecessors, though. She was also the Emmys’ first emcee not known primarily for her comedy work since 2008, when the five first-ever nominees in a new reality-TV-hosting category, including Ryan Seacrest and Heidi Klum, delivered what critics agreed was one of the worst ceremonies in history. Hargitay is, of course, synonymous with Olivia Benson, the heroic sex-crimes investigator she has portrayed in NBC’s Law & Order: SVU since its 1999 premiere. What casual fans might not have known is that she trained with legendary improv troupe the Groundlings and appeared in sitcoms like Seinfeld; “all I wanted to be was you,” she told Amy Poehler on the latter’s podcast, Good Hang. Hargitay also went into the gig with lots of goodwill from audiences inside the Peacock Theater and at home. Not only is she an advocate for survivors of sexual violence, on screen and off; she’s also in the midst of a pop-culture renaissance, having won two Emmys for the documentary she directed about her famous mom, Jayne Mansfield, conquered the one-woman play Every Brilliant Thing on Broadway, and endeared herself to Knicks fans as a stalwart supporter on the sidelines of this year’s NBA Finals. In light of all this, Variety dubbed her “a national best friend.” Maybe that explains why Hargitay was just kinda there with us throughout the ceremony, more an affable companion than a genuine ringmaster. In a monologue that was practically over before it started, she quipped about how many of the stars in the room she’d “arrested” in their guest appearances as SVU’s twisted perp of the week. There was a sendup of Nicole Kidman’s five-year-old viral AMC Theatres commercial, featuring an interjection (“That’s my line”) from Kidman herself. It wasn’t the only joke of the night that felt stale (see: Hargitay griping that the Game of Thrones furs she donned to introduce two of the franchise’s stars were “hot as balls”). Minutes after Hargitay joked that male hosts had dominated the Emmys for so long because “for 15 straight years, Amy and Tina said no,” Poehler presented an award with a joke about the night’s losers outnumbering its winners that surpassed anything in the monologue. She was only the first scene stealer in a program full of them—and I’m not even talking about the many deserving winners, to whom the Emmys do rightly belong. (It does seem worth noting that Writing for a Drama Series winner Vince Gilligan made the strongest political statement of a telecast whose scripted portions steered conspicuously clear of controversy, urging Americans to show each other respect and “stop listening to a relative few who tell us that that is for suckers and who get their power from dividing us.”) Presenting the award for Lead Actor in a Drama Series, frequent awards-show MVP John Mulaney eclipsed the monologue in both humor and stage time with a tongue-in-cheek celebration of men that ultimately had him reading from A Guy’s Guide to Being a Man’s Man by Frank Vincent. Andrew Rannells swooped in with a burst of musical theater energy to save Hargitay’s shaky song about canceled series. The host didn’t even get to own the obligatory pre-taped SVU parody, whose mock-investigation into whether Taylor Swift would attend the Emmys landed as a flimsy excuse to get the most famous entertainer on Earth (who is also famously an SVU superfan and Hargitay pal) on screen. All told, it was neither an excruciating Emmys nor a great one. There were certainly some lovely moments. The Catherine O’Hara tribute from her on-screen children Macaulay Culkin, Annie Murphy, and Dan Levy stood out as moving in its genuine warmth and specificity. More often, opportunities to honor a beloved person or show were squandered; was Sarah Michelle Gellar and David Boreanaz presenting an award together really the exciting 30th anniversary Buffy the Vampire Slayer reunion fans were anticipating? Similarly, Hargitay didn’t embarrass herself. Many of the flaws in her performance should be blamed on lackluster writing, from a non sequitur segue after her self-deprecating Ghoulies flashback to that unforgivable “Hannah Mammogramma” pun. She may have failed to match the Levys’ energy or belatedly prove herself as a great comic talent, but at least she avoided a Bargatze- or Seacrest-level disaster and came out with her likability intact. Regardless of whether she gets invited back, here’s to the Emmys giving women more chances to be wonderful, awful, and even just-OK Emmys hosts.

Who Is Umar Kremlev? The Russian Businessman With Ties to Putin Who Partly Funded Trump Jr.’s Wedding Celebrations
2026-09-14

Who Is Umar Kremlev? The Russian Businessman With Ties to Putin Who Partly Funded Trump Jr.’s Wedding Celebrations

Update your preferences in Account Settings Russian President Vladimir Putin (L) greets the president of the International Boxing Association Umar Kremlev (R) during a meeting with national boxing team at the Kremlin in Moscow, Russia, on April 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A Russian businessman with ties to the country’s President Vladimir Putin footed the bill for celebrations following the wedding of President Donald Trump’s son, Donald Trump Jr., Trump Jr. and his wife, Bettina, have confirmed. Oligarch Umar Kremlev’s contributions to the wedding festivities were first reported by ProPublica, which wrote that the Russian businessman spent hundreds of thousands of dollars to rent out a private island in the Bahamas and a fireworks show. The outlet reported that the guest list included roughly 50 guests, including family members, close friends, and Kremlev, who according to ProPublica was part of a larger group of Russian guests whose presence was a source of confusion for other attendees. Trump Jr. and Bettina Trump shared a statement on social media in response to criticism over his financial involvement in the event, defending what she described as an “extraordinarily generous wedding gift from a friend” and denying any political motive behind Kremlev’s attendance. “Our dear friend Umar very generously hosted two incredible nights of celebrations for us AFTER our wedding,” they wrote in a post on Bettina Trump’s Instagram on Monday in a caption that accompanied a series of photos from the evenings in question showing the couple partying with other guests, performances, and the beach-side venue. “It’s unfortunate that something so personal and happy can be recast as something political or sinister simply because of who someone is or where they come from. Friendship doesn’t require a political motive.” The Trumps’ connection to a figure closely tied to the leadership in Russia—and his apparently lavish financial contribution to the wedding celebrations—has however drawn concerns from Democratic politicians and experts in ethics and intelligence, who have raised questions about what might be expected in return for the "gift" and warned that Russian intelligence services may seek to weaponize such a relationship. Here’s what to know about Kremlev and his ties to Putin and the Trump family. Kremlev, 43, is a native of Tajikistan. He was previously known by his surname at birth, Lutfulloyev, which he changed to Kremlev in 2010 when he was in his late twenties, according to the independent Russian news organization Proekt. Prior to that name change, Kremlev was convicted of extortion in 2004 and battery in 2007. He went on to join a notorious biker gang, the Night Wolves, not long after changing his name, according to Russian news reports. Outlets reported that during his time in the group, which was the first official biker community in the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics (USSR), Kremlev met Alexei Rubezhnoi, who currently leads Putin’s presidential security service. The Washington Post reported that Putin himself is considered an honorary member of the Night Wolves. In 2015, Kremlev, who had boxed since he was a child, founded a company, Patriot, that promoted boxing matches. Two years later, he became the secretary general of the International Boxing Association (IBA) after a shake up led to Rubezhnoi becoming the chairman of its supervisory board. Rubezhnoi then nominated Kremlev to the secretary general position; from there, Kremlev went on to be elected as president of the IBA in 2020. In 2024, Kremlev acquired Rolf, Russia’s largest car dealership group, which had previously been state-owned. “These services,” Triplett says in reference to the Kremlin, “are exceptional at finding pain points and then and then manipulating them, and you can get people into difficult positions.” Kremlev has been connected with Putin for years, as has been documented through public appearances and business dealings. During Kremlev’s time as IBA president, Gazprom, a state-owned energy company and one of the largest companies in Russia, became a financial backer of the boxing federation. That same year, Kremlev stood alongside Putin during the opening of the International Boxing Center in Moscow. Under Putin, many Russian government officials or oligarchs serve in supervisory positions for sports organizations in the country. “Umar is guided by Putin. It was using the sport for soft political power,” an ex-IBA board member told ProPublica of Kremlev’s leadership of the organization. “It’s geopolitics. That it’s boxing is just happenstance.” Following Kremlev’s acquisition of Rolf in 2024, meanwhile, Putin repealed the company’s federal ownership, which he had established through a presidential decree in 2023. He was also honored by Putin with the Order of Friendship, according to the IBA, a state accolade that celebrates those who have helped to improve the Russian Federation in various sectors. And earlier this year, Kemlev accompanied Putin on a visit to China, according to Chinese state media. Kremlev’s press office told ProPublica in a statement that he was not part of the Russian President’s “official delegation” during the trip. Kremlev has been publicly linked to President Trump and Trump Jr. through the IBA in recent months. It’s unclear how else he may be connected with the Trump family. After President Trump returned to the White House last year, Kremlev and the CEO of the boxing organization sent him an open letter congratulating him on his re-election and expressing the IBA’s “sincere gratitude for your strong and principled stance regarding the inadmissibility in the participation of ineligible athletes in women’s boxing competition,” in reference to Trump’s stance against the participation of transgender women in women’s sports. The letter then nodded towards the 2028 Olympic Games, which will be hosted by the U.S. in Los Angeles, saying that the IBA looks ahead “with great optimism to the possibility of working together to make the Olympic movement great again, advance the sport of boxing and to champion the rights of athletes globally.” Last September, Trump Jr. sat on a panel discussion hosted by the IBA in Istanbul, during which he also critiqued the participation of transgender women in women’s sports while onstage with Kremlev. Kremlev’s press office said in its statement to ProPublica that he and Trump Jr. initially met “a couple years ago” and that the two men “have a friendly relationship.” It added that Kremlev “has never discussed political matters with any of his American friends and acquaintances,” Trump Jr. included. A spokesperson for Trump Jr. told the New York Timesthat the two men bonded over boxing and hunting after meeting through a mutual friend, and that they are not in business with one another. The President, who did not attend Trump Jr.’s wedding or the following celebrations, said in a statement provided to the Times on Monday that “I have no idea who Umar is, never heard of him, and he didn’t pay for Don and Bettina’s wedding, which took place at a totally different location, and on a different day.” A spokeswoman for Eric Trump, Trump Jr.’s brother, meanwhile told the Times and ProPublica, “Eric has absolutely no clue who this person is.” “If the allegations are true, then it certainly reflects poor judgment on Donald Trump Jr.’s part,” Dan Hoffman, an intelligence expert with over 30 years of experience working in the Central Intelligence Agency (CIA), U.S. military, Department of State, and Department of Commerce, tells TIME. “It is highly unlikely Umar Kremlev would have any interaction with any member of the Trump family without the Kremlin knowing about it beforehand.” Holden Triplett, who served nearly 15 years with the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and was previously the Director for Counterintelligence at the National Security Council at the White House during Trump’s first Administration, says that any kind of relationship between an individual with ties to the President and someone linked to the Kremlin can pose a risk to the U.S.—though he says he doesn’t find the Trumps’ apparent relationship with Kremlev to be particularly alarming. “Even the most above-board, non-threatening-nefarious relationship could be weaponized by intelligence services,” he tells TIME. “Russians commonly try to use, as a lot of intel agencies do, business relationships as a non-alarming way to establish connection with someone … It is one that the Russians have used with lots of, or attempts at least, with lots of other individuals in politics.” This isn’t the first time Trump Jr. has drawn controversy over interactions related to Russia. During the 2016 presidential campaign, Trump Jr. met with a Russian lawyer who claimed she could provide information that would hurt his father’s Democratic opponent Hillary Clinton’s campaign. That meeting later became a point in the investigation special counsel Robert Mueller conducted into President Trump’s campaign and its ties to Russia, though Trump Jr. was never accused of wrongdoing in the probe.

Mitch McConnell Returns to the Senate, Ending His Months-Long Absence

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sen. Mitch McConnell (R-KY) speaks to the press as he arrives for a vote at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 14, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sen. Mitch McConnell announced that he’s casting a vote in the Senate on Monday, marking the end of his months-long absence from the upper chamber after he was hospitalized in June following a fall. “Today, I’ll cast my first Senate vote since I took a bad fall back in June,” McConnell said in a post on X on Monday evening. “I’m really looking forward to being back on the Senate floor and seeing my colleagues.” The 84-year-old Kentucky Republican went on to say that his "recovery has been a long and often frustrating process,” adding that the “lingering effects” of his bout with polio during his childhood have also impacted his recovery. “I’m still not quite back to 100%, but I’ve assured [Senate Majority Leader John Thune] that, as I continue with physical therapy on the advice of my doctors, I will do my best to be present for tough votes when our Conference needs me,” McConnell said. McConnell’s office initially shared little to no information when the Senator was hospitalized on June 14. After weeks of uncertainty—and his absence from the Senate—rumors and speculation over the state of his health began swirling online. In July, Democratic Kentucky Gov. Andy Beshear shared a letter in which he urged McConnell to “fully update Kentuckians regarding the current status of [his] health.” Read More: Kentucky Gov. Beshear Keeps Pushing for Info on McConnell’s Health. He Says He’s ‘Heard Absolutely Nothing Back’ A few days later, the Republican lawmaker disclosed that he had been taken to the hospital after he “briefly” lost consciousness following a fall, and that he later had to grapple with a “mild case of pneumonia.” He pushed back, though, on rumors that he was suffering from any severe conditions, saying that his doctors confirmed that he didn’t break any bones or suffer a concussion, and that he didn’t have any tumors or experience a heart attack. At the end of July, McConnell’s office shared another update, stating that the Senator was maintaining his physical therapy, per his doctors’ orders, and that he had been discharged from the hospital but was not, at that time, medically cleared to leave the rehabilitation center he was at and return to the upper chamber. On Aug. 6, his office announced that he had been discharged from the rehabilitation facility that day and would continue his recovery at home. McConnell plans to retire in January, when his term comes to an end. This is not the first time that he has had a health scare; he has had multiple falling incidents in recent years.

Canada Seeks 'Unique Alliance' With E.U. Amid U.S. Trade War. Here's What It Could Look Like
2026-09-14

Canada Seeks 'Unique Alliance' With E.U. Amid U.S. Trade War. Here's What It Could Look Like

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen and Canada's Prime Minister Mark Carney speak at the G7 summit in Evian-les-Bains, France, on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said Canada is seeking a “unique alliance” with the European Union as he aims to strengthen international ties amid a deepening trade war with the U.S. “What we are looking, and will begin discussions for, [is] a unique alliance with the European Union,” Carney told reporters at the Toronto International Film Festival on Sunday. “We share the same values, we have the same priorities, and we have very complementary strengths.” Canada’s European pivot has been months in the making, following growing tensions between Carney’s government and the Trump Administration. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect last week, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. after negotiations collapsed last month. Carney has encouraged Canadians to take back economic power at the kitchen table, and many have embraced a boycott of U.S. products. Trump has also repeatedly threatened Canada’s sovereignty, speaking openly of his desire to annex the country and make it the “51st state.” The U.S. accounted for 72.5% of Canadian merchandise exports in 2025, according to the country's annual trade report, making it Canada’s largest trading partner. But Carney is keen to build an international support network that isn’t reliant on the U.S. Over the past 18 months since Carney entered office, Canada has signed agreements that cover trade, defense, more with countries such as Indonesia, Japan, and Ecuador. At the E.U.-Canada summit in June 2025, Canada established a new Security and Defense Partnership with the bloc. “Our partnership is grounded in the core values we share: democracy, human rights, the rule of law, and open, rules-based markets," read a joint statement marking the occasion. In January, Carney notably met with China’s President Xi Jinping to forge what he referred to as a “new strategic partnership” to end Canada's economic reliance on the American market. Carney agreed to cut his country’s 100% tariff on Chinese electric cars in return for lower tariffs on Canadian farm products, including canola seeds, a major Canadian export. Continuing with these efforts, Carney on Monday brought together executives and senior leaders from global investment firms to encourage them to invest in the country at the Canada Investment Summit in Toronto. He’s heading for a three-day trip to Europe on Tuesday, where he’ll meet with various European leaders including British Prime Minister Andy Burnham. He’s also set to address the European Parliament in Strasbourg, France, on Thursday, in what could symbolize the mark of a new era between Canada and Europe. Here’s a look at the alliance options on the table between Canada and the bloc, and what that could mean for U.S.-Canada relations. Canada is not looking to join the E.U. as a member, Carney clarified amid questions over what a firmer relationship between his country and the 27-member bloc might look like. “We’re not looking to become a member,” Carney said, emphasizing he’s instead keen to form an alliance. “We’re stronger together, and this is at a time where the world’s more dangerous and divided, and friends need to stick together in these times.” Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union explicitly states that any “European State” can apply for membership, making Canada ineligible. However, a report in the Wall Street Journal, quoting officials from both sides, said Carney is keen for Canada to become an “associate member” of the E.U. When approached for comment, the European Commission directed TIME to remarks made during a Monday press briefing in Brussels. “Canada is a strategically important partner for the European Union, and indeed a close friend,” the European Commission’s deputy chief spokesperson Olof Gill told reporters. “We deeply value this co-operation. You will recall that at our E.U.-Canada summit last year, we set a very high level of ambition for cooperation across the board, this year our ambition is even higher.” When asked directly what discussions had taken place between Canadian and European officials, or what this “unique alliance” could look like, Gill declined to answer. TIME has reached out to the Canadian Prime Minister’s office for comment. Frank Schimmelfennig, a professor of European politics at ETH Zurich, predicts that Canada may wish to pursue deeper cooperation through additional trade agreements, or take inspiration from countries that have close relationships with the bloc without being E.U. members. “Canada is already one of the most integrated non-European countries. It has the densest network of institutional relationships with the E.U.,” he tells TIME, referencing the Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement (CETA), a major free-trade deal between the E.U. and Canada, signed in 2016, and Horizon Europe, an initiative on collaborative research launched by the European Union in 2021 and joined by Canada in 2024. “Canada could have more of the same, which means additional sectoral agreements for sectors that haven't been covered yet,” he says. “That would basically be a lateral extension of the current relationship. Anything more, would mean access to the internal market which would require an arrangement like the EEA.” The European Economic Area (EEA) includes countries such as Iceland and Norway, which are not members of the E.U., and provides for the free movement of goods, services, and people within the E.U.’s member states, while enabling cooperation across multiple industries. “The EEA countries have access to the E.U. internal market in return for adopting the relevant E.U. regulations and directives, so that would be a much denser legal integration than what Canada has now,” says Schimmelfennig. At the World Economic Forum in Davos in January, Carney declared a "rupture" in the international rules-based order. He said "great powers" were using tariffs and financial systems as instruments of coercion. He did not directly name Trump or the U.S. The speech came at a moment of acute transatlantic tension over Trump’s push to annex the semi-autonomous Danish territory of Greenland and his short-lived threats to tariff European allies until Denmark relented. Patrick Leblond, an associate professor at the University of Ottawa, says Carney’s movements now are in response to the world “seeing a United States that is becoming more protectionist, nationalist, and willing to use its leverage points to gain concessions, including on very close allies like Canada and Europeans.” “For Canada, [the consensus is] we need to do more with partners who share our values based on liberal democracy, open markets, rule of law, international agreements, and the first port of call for Carney is the European Union,” Leblond tells TIME. These trade efforts, he continues, may allow Carney to “build momentum, and hopefully bring other countries” into a broader coalition of what the Canadian leader has often called “middle powers." In July, Carney, in a joint op-ed with Finland’s President Alexander Stubb, urged the “middle powers of the world” to “unite.” Championing middle power countries that possess significant economic power and diplomatic influence, but lack market or military power, the leaders implored: “In a rapidly changing world, we have the grit and the sisu to chart the path ahead—and we know that so have many others whom we invite to join us on this quest.” Leblod explains that “ultimately trust has been eroded” with the U.S. and Canada must re-think its place in the wider global economy in the long-term. “The first time we thought Trump was a blimp, but he's not, and we're looking at what's happening in terms of democracy and political polarization, and a lot of Canadians think we can't rely on the U.S.,” he says. According to a poll by Nanos Research, conducted between Aug. 30 and Sept. 2, 74% of Canadians disagreed that “the U.S. is a trustworthy ally of Canada.” An earlier poll by Angus Reid Institute, conducted between July 10-14, found that 42% of respondents said they think the Canadian government should approach the U.S. as an enemy/potential threat. The E.U. has also clashed with Trump over tariffs, his Greenland annexation threats, and the wider split between the U.S. President and several European leaders. German Chancellor Friedrich Merz in February warned of “a deep rift” between Europe and the United States, arguing that the latter “will not be powerful enough to go it alone.” But relations have since soured further amid the fallout of the Iran war, particularly between Merz and Trump. Meanwhile, Canada has also embraced Europe in the cultural space. It was announced earlier this year that Canada will ​join the Eurovision Song Contest in 2027, becoming the first new ⁠participant since Australia in 2015. Carney’s highly-anticipated speech at the European Parliament in Strasbourg this week stems from an invitation extended by European Parliament president Roberta Metsola. Leblond predicts Carney will use the speech “to reiterate a lot of the things he mentioned at Davos,” but with a strong, intentional focus on the E.U. “I would expect that he will use that address to call on the E.U. to work with Canada to push this idea of co-operation beyond the North Atlantic and include partners and allies in the Indo-Pacific,” he says. The Canadian leader is also expected to be a guest at the State of the European Union debate, where European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen is due to deliver a speech outlining the Commission's work and plans for the future. Von der Leyen called E.U.–Canada ties “one of the closest partnerships in the world” when she celebrated 50 years of E.U. diplomatic presence in Canada in May. She declared the two sides were “closer than ever” as they confronted wars, an energy crisis and “new fractures in old alliances.” Schimmelfennig says Canada inching closer to the E.U. is a strong display of “how both partners are affected by the deteriorating transatlantic relationship” with the U.S. “They feel that they need to move together because the world is getting more dangerous, and more difficult for both [parties],” he says.

In Gen Z Romance Shows Like Crew Girl, Sports Are the New Sex
2026-09-14

In Gen Z Romance Shows Like Crew Girl, Sports Are the New Sex

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings From left: Sam Braun, Miku Martineau, and Kyle Clark in Crew Girl Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the Netflix teen drama Crew Girl, 16-year-old solo rowing champ Teagan Tao has her life uprooted when her hotel-magnate dad gets a tip that he’s about to be arrested for financial crimes and disappears. Suddenly, Teagan (Miku Martineau) and her flighty mom, Ella (Jessica Paré), are broke and moving across the country to the crumbling New England house where Ella grew up. The town is all clam shacks and marinas—and the prestigious Easton Prep, where Teagan matriculates thanks to an education trust that was somehow spared from the seizure of the Taos’ assets. The only problem, besides the rampant snobbery, is that Eaton has no girls’ crew team. Forced to coxswain for the boys, Teagan starts falling for two of her teammates: kind, working-class townie Cam Dillinger (Kyle Clark) and bratty airline scion Josh Regis (Sam Braun), who’s locked in a premature succession battle with his cousin Ryder (Connor Paton). Meanwhile, a gay rower, Baloo (Jude Wilson), is secretly hooking up with a guy on a rival team. All of which is to say that Crew Girl, whose first season is now the no. 1 show on Netflix, plays like a greatest-hits compilation of teen romance tropes. First among the classics is a love triangle (see: Twilight, Riverdale, The Vampire Diaries, My So-Called Life). As is often the case, this one is enhanced by the quaintness of its small-town setting (Dawson’s Creek, One Tree Hill) and complicated by both wealth disparities (Gossip Girl, Pretty in Pink) and one character’s fish-out-of-water, new-kid-in-school status (The O.C., Beverly Hills, 90210). There’s also a youthful mom (you might remember Paré as Mad Men’s Megan) doing a painfully slow will-they-won’t-they dance with an old friend (Thomas Cadrot), à la Gilmore Girls’ Lorelai and Luke. From left: Jarrett Carlington, Jude Wilson, Connor Paton, Kyle Clark, and Miku Martineau in Crew Girl Netflix But what’s remarkable about Crew Girl—which is, despite an effervescent lead and a few fun storylines built around eccentric minor crew-boy characters, otherwise decidedly mid—is how comprehensively it showcases the conventions unique to the genre’s newest generation. TV has, in the last few years, capitalized on publishing’s pandemic-era romance boom, churning out love stories tailored to the young, female audience driving authors like Emily Henry and Ali Hazelwood up best-seller lists. Often, that means adapting popular YA or New Adult novels; Prime Video’s success with Jenny Han’s coming-of-age, beach-town love triangle The Summer I Turned Pretty paved the way for Netflix’s take on Ali Novak’s My Life with the Walter Boys as well as XO, Kitty, Han’s spinoff of her book-to-film franchise To All the Boys I’ve Loved Before. Queer romance, mainly of the gay male variety, from Netflix’s tender and chaste Heartstopper to the spicy Canadian phenomenon Heated Rivalry, which streams on HBO Max in the U.S., has also jumped from page to screen. (Crew Girl isn’t an adaptation, but a novelization released in tandem with the show suggests it’s meant to feel like one.) This year has seen a particular glut of new titles, from Netflix’s figure-skating drama Finding Her Edge to Prime’s Off Campus and Every Year After. The latter platform even scored a hit in August with an exceptional original teen romance, Sterling Point, proving the niche isn’t entirely dependent on preexisting IP. These shows have all the love triangles and class struggles and outsider angst that have defined youthful romance tales since Jane Austen was writing them, capturing eternal adolescent fantasies (being desirable enough to have two people fighting over you) and fears (not measuring up to the rich kids) alike. Pretty appears to have sparked a mini-trend of siblings competing for the same crush, a trope that also pops up in Every Year After, Walter Boys, and Finding Her Edge. The fraught post-DEI-backlash politics of representation, especially for the most racially and ethnically diverse generation on record, are evident in the shows’ leads. Like Crew Girl’s Martineau, many are multiracial but play characters with vaguely defined backgrounds who live with either a single white parent or a white surrogate family. (New York magazine just spotlighted the "Wasian generation," a long list of up-and-coming white and Asian stars, citing Heated Rivalry’s Hudson Williams, Pretty’s Lola Tung, and XO, Kitty’s Anna Cathcart among other white and Asian stars.) Liberal and minority viewers get to see mixed-race families and romances, and a TV landscape that isn’t all white. The anti-”woke” crowd is spared identity politics, non-English dialogue, and often all other forms of cultural specificity. Savvy streamers avoid backlash from both sides. Connor Storrie, left, and Hudson Williams in Heated Rivalry HBO Max What’s most striking about these series, though, are the novel ways in which they embrace physicality. With the steamy exceptions of Heated Rivalry and Off Campus (both geared toward a slightly older age range), the sexual content is pretty PG-13. Objectification is kept to a minimum, at least by characters we’re supposed to like. Yet bodies are conspicuously activated, in what feel like pointed responses to—and, for young viewers, escape hatches from—digital disembodiment. Summer romances, and specifically those that bring together wealthy vacationers and the locals who work for them, aren’t new to fiction. It’s the prominence of nature in the recent crop of teen romances (and teen hits in other genres, like Netflix’s Outer Banks) that makes an impression. As it toggles between protagonist Percy’s (Sadie Soverall) life in her late 20s and a sequence of idyllic teenage lake-house summers glimpsed in flashbacks, Every Year After uses its setting to visualize two brothers’ rivalry for her affection. In one recurring image, Percy sits at the edge of her house’s dock, looking on as sensitive Sam (Matt Cornett) and arrogant Charlie (Michael Bradway) shove each other off a slippery wooden raft. Sterling Point brings a high-strung New York City girl (Ella Rubin) to an island in Canada’s Muskoka region, a paradise of lush, lakefront forests. Her rich-vs.-poor-boy love triangle plays out over lazy days spent boating, swimming, and sunbathing on rocky beaches. Cellphone service is conveniently spotty. An abundance of sports romances make such physicality even more explicit. In Off Campus and Heated Rivalry, the boys who serve as eye candy for any viewer attracted to men are always slamming into each other on the ice and changing together in the locker room. In Finding Her Edge, a show about young Olympics hopefuls at a residential training center, an ice-dancing duo is bound to become a love affair (as long as the guy is into girls). Even the neurotic hero of Heartstopper (Joe Locke) finds first love on the rugby field. Crew Girl casts many thirsty gazes on the musclebound upper bodies of Teagan’s teammates. These stories aren’t just about the sweaty sensuality of athletes in motion, though. They’re about ambition and passion and the competitive spirit as romantic accelerants—about how the shared commitment to spend hours every day striving for excellence while one’s peers are screwing around online can bond people. Belmont Cameli and Ella Bright in Off Campus Liane Hentscher—Prime Why use athletics as a fig leaf for sex in the uncensored age of streaming, years after shows like Euphoria and Skins obliterated the notion that TV for teens should be wholesome? Maybe because modesty is in. Gen Z’s squeamishness about sex on screen has fueled successive waves of hand-wringing among their elders. The so-called sex recession plaguing this cohort in real life has been duly noted, with suggested explanations ranging from extreme onlineness in general to poor socialization during the COVID years of Zoom school and virtual prom in particular. The thing is, biologically speaking, teens are the same jumble of hormones in 2026 that they were during the Summer of Love. “Many of them are very horny,” Carter Sherman, the author of The Second Coming: Sex and the Next Generation's Fight Over Its Future, assured WIRED last year. “They would like to be having sex, and in fact they feel a lot of shame over the fact that they haven't had sex yet or that they’re not having sex enough.” As transitional objects to ease anxieties around physical intimacy, swimsuits and erg machines make a lot of sense. The most salient takeaway from Crew Girl and its ilk might be buried in a less eye-catching finding of 2025’s Teens and Screens, the annual UCLA report on young people’s attitudes toward entertainment media that reliably stokes the “Gen Z hates sex scenes” panic. Relatability is the new watchword. Whereas millennials wallowed in wealth porn, took vicarious flight with superheroes, and sucked the life out of vampire content, today’s adolescents ranked “people with lives like mine” as the No. 1 element they’re looking for in TV shows and movies. Although they’re aspirational enough to feel escapist, with their summer houses and prep-school popularity contests, this season’s teen romances reflect that shift. For a generation raised in the sensory deprivation tank of the internet, whose fears of infinite digital liminality just gave us the horror movies Backrooms and It Ends, the fantasy is humbler than superpowers. It’s being grounded in physical space, a human body capable of sharing tactile experiences with other embodied people. Sex is one way to make those connections. Crew is another.

How The Early Spring Ends—And Sets the Stage for More Chinese Dramas on Netflix
